RQ4 – Operational Viability: Does PQ-TLS introduce measurable performance, reliability, or compatibility constraints in operational Internet deployments?

## 4.5 Security Co-evolution

While the surveyed policies provide guidance on which cryptographic algorithms to adopt and when to transition, they focus primarily on primitive-level replacement. How these algorithms should be inte<sub>g</sub>rated into Internet-facin<sub>g p</sub>rotocols such as TLS is t<sub>yp</sub>icall<sub>y</sub> left im<sub>p</sub>licit or deferred to existin<sub>g</sub> securit<sub>y</sub> baselines and operational practices, which may evolve independently of PQ transition <sub>p</sub>lannin<sub>g</sub> (e.<sub>g</sub>., [77, 81]).

H<sub>oweve</sub>r<sub>, p</sub>ri<sub>o</sub>r Int<sub>e</sub>rn<sub>e</sub>t-<sub>sca</sub>l<sub>e</sub> m<sub>easu</sub>r<sub>e</sub>m<sub>e</sub>nt <sub>s</sub>t<sub>u</sub>di<sub>es</sub> h<sub>ave co</sub>n<sub>s</sub>i<sub>s</sub> t<sub>en</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> <sub>s</sub>h<sub>own</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>pro</sub>t<sub>oco</sub>l<sub>-</sub>l<sub>eve</sub>l <sub>secur</sub>it<sub>y</sub> d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>s</sub> <sub>no</sub>t <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>on</sub> <sub>cryp</sub> to<sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hic <sub>p</sub>rimitives but also on confi<sub>g</sub>uration h<sub>yg</sub>iene<sub>,</sub> includin<sub>g</sub> su<sub>pp</sub>orted <sub>p</sub>rotocol versions<sub>,</sub> ci<sub>p</sub>her suites<sub>,</sub> certificate mana<sub>g</sub>ement <sub>p</sub>ractices, and ex<sub>p</sub>osure to le<sub>g</sub>ac<sub>y</sub> vulnerabilities [1, 12, 53].

As PQ-TLS deployment progresses, an open question is whether PQ adoption occurs alongside broader security modernization, or whether PQ mechanisms are layered onto existing configurations without corres<sub>p</sub>ondin<sub>g</sub> im<sub>p</sub>rovements in overall <sub>p</sub>rotocol securit<sub>y</sub>. Em<sub>p</sub>irical evaluation of this relationshi<sub>p</sub> is necessar<sub>y</sub> to understand whether observable PQ adoption reflects holistic security advancement or isolated cr<sub>yp</sub>to<sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hic substitution.

RQ5 – Security Co-evolution: Does PQ-TLS adoption coincide with broader improvements in protocol-level security posture?

To examine whether observed PQ-TLS adoption reflects independent o<sub>p</sub>erator mi<sub>g</sub>ration or infrastructure-driven de<sub>p</sub>lo<sub>y</sub>ment<sub>,</sub> we attribute each domain to the entit<sub>y</sub> res<sub>p</sub>onsible for terminatin<sub>g</sub> its TLS connections and subse<sub>q</sub>uentl<sub>y</sub> infer who is <sub>p</sub>otentiall<sub>y</sub> res<sub>p</sub>onsible for mana<sub>g</sub>in<sub>g</sub> its TLS confi<sub>g</sub>uration.

W<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t id<sub>en</sub>tif<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>ras</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ure</sub> <sub>prov</sub>id<sub>er</sub> b<sub>e</sub>hi<sub>n</sub>d <sub>eac</sub>h d<sub>oma</sub>i<sub>n</sub> usin<sub>g</sub> a la<sub>y</sub>ered resolution strate<sub>gy</sub>. We be<sub>g</sub>in b<sub>y</sub> matchin<sub>g</sub> the resolved IP address a<sub>g</sub>ainst known CDN and cloud <sub>p</sub>rovider ran<sub>g</sub>es (e.<sub>g</sub>., Cloudflare<sup>2</sup>, Fastl<sub>y</sub><sup>3</sup>). If no match is found, we anal<sub>y</sub>ze DNS CNAME chains, reverse DNS (PTR) looku<sub>p</sub>s, HTTP(S) res<sub>p</sub>onse metadata<sub>,</sub> and WHOIS records for <sub>p</sub>rovider-s<sub>p</sub>ecific <sub>p</sub>atterns. When these si<sub>g</sub>nals are inconclusive, we use autonomous s<sub>y</sub>stem (AS) <sub>owners</sub>hi<sub>p</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>ti<sub>on as a</sub> f<sub>a</sub>llb<sub>ac</sub>k<sub>.</sub>

Given the inferred infrastr<sub>u</sub>ct<sub>u</sub>re <sub>p</sub>rovider<sub>,</sub> we then estimate whether TLS confi<sub>g</sub>uration is mana<sub>g</sub>ed b<sub>y</sub> the <sub>p</sub>rovider or b<sub>y</sub> the domain o<sub>p</sub>erator followin<sub>g</sub> [51]. Domains associated with known mana<sub>g</sub>ed infrastructure <sub>p</sub>latforms (e.<sub>g</sub>., CDNs), associated with <sub>p</sub>latform-as-a-service oferin<sub>g</sub>s (e.<sub>g</sub>., <sub>g</sub>ithub.io, blo<sub>g</sub>s<sub>p</sub>ot.com), or exhibiti<sub>ng c</sub>h<sub>arac</sub>t<sub>er</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>cs o</sub>f <sub>s</sub>h<sub>are</sub>d h<sub>os</sub>ti<sub>ng suc</sub>h <sub>as</sub> hi<sub>g</sub>h IP f<sub>anou</sub>t <sub>an</sub>d known infrastructure-linked metadata, are labeled as potentially infrastructure provider managed. In contrast, domains with charact<sub>er</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>cs</sub> <sub>o</sub>f d<sub>e</sub>di<sub>ca</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d h<sub>os</sub>ti<sub>ng,</sub> i<sub>nc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> l<sub>ow</sub> f<sub>anou</sub>t<sub>,</sub> d<sub>oma</sub>i<sub>n-a</sub>li<sub>gne</sub>d certificates are labeled as potentially owner managed. Domains that cannot be reliably classified are labeled as unknown.

This framework <sub>p</sub>rovides <sub>p</sub>robabilistic attribution<sub>,</sub> enablin<sub>g</sub> us to distinguish between deployments potentially driven by infrast<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ure</sub> <sub>p</sub>l<sub>a</sub>tf<sub>orms</sub> <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>ose</sub> <sub>manage</sub>d di<sub>rec</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> b<sub>y</sub> <sub>serv</sub>i<sub>ce</sub> <sub>opera</sub>t<sub>ors.</sub> F<sub>u</sub>rther details and validation are <sub>p</sub>rovided in A<sub>pp</sub>endix C.

## 6.1 Configuration Landscape

Across the stable panel, we do not observe any successful nego tiation of PQ signature algorithms. This absence is consistent across all vanta<sub>g</sub>e <sub>p</sub>oints and measurement rounds<sub>,</sub> indicatin<sub>g</sub> that PQ signature deployment remains negligible in publicly reachable TLS services durin<sub>g</sub> the stud<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>eriod. This findin<sub>g</sub> ali<sub>g</sub>ns with the urgent transition focus on PQ key establishment relative to authentication (A<sub>pp</sub>endix 2.6). Conse<sub>q</sub>uentl<sub>y</sub>, the remainder of our analysis focuses on PQ key establishment, which represent the only observable form of PQC deployment in operational TLS endpoints <sup>at</sup> p<sup>resent.</sup>

Key Takeaways for RQ1: Despite diverse policy recommendations, operational PQ-TLS has efectively standardized on a single hybrid key exchange construction while signature algorithms are absent in deployment. Observed adoption reflects a narrow set of interoperable default configurations, rather than the broader range of algorithmic options described in PQC transition guidelines.

## 6.2 Deployment Agency

To assess whether observed PQ-TLS adoption reflects independent o<sub>p</sub>erator mi<sub>g</sub>ration or infrastructure-driven de<sub>p</sub>lo<sub>y</sub>ment<sub>,</sub> we a<sub>pp</sub>l<sub>y</sub> the TLS mana<sub>g</sub>ement attribution methodolo<sub>gy</sub> described in § 5.4. Based on this analysis, we classify each domain as potentially infrastructure provider managed, potentially owner managed, or unknown, reflectin<sub>g</sub> the inferred res<sub>p</sub>onsibilit<sub>y</sub> for TLS confi<sub>g</sub>uration.

Amon<sub>g</sub> the 684,494 domains in the stable <sub>p</sub>anel, 416,669 (60.87%) <sub>are</sub> <sub>a</sub>tt<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> <sub>po</sub>t<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>ras</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ure</sub> <sub>prov</sub>id<sub>er</sub> <sub>manage</sub>d d<sub>e-</sub> <sub>p</sub>lo<sub>y</sub>ments, 205,960 (30.09%) are <sub>p</sub>otentiall<sub>y</sub> owner mana<sub>g</sub>ed, and 61,865 (9.04%) remain unattributed.

By March 2026, 93.92% of observed PQ-TLS deployments originate from infrastructure provider managed configura tions. In contrast, only 4.58% of observed PQ-TLS deployments were owner managed, with the majority continuing to negotiate <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l TLS<sub>.</sub>

Fi <sub>ure</sub> 1 f<sub>ur</sub>th<sub>er</sub> ill<sub>us</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>es</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> i<sub>m</sub>b<sub>a</sub>l<sub>ance w</sub>ithi<sub>n a</sub>tt<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on ca</sub>t<sub>e-</sub> <sub>g</sub>ories. More than 75% of infrastructure <sub>p</sub>rovider mana<sub>g</sub>ed domains support PQ-TLS, compared to less than 10% of owner managed domains. Taken together, these disparities indicate that the majority of observable PQ-TLS adoption is driven by platform-level configuration changes rather than deliberate migration by individual service operators.

To further examine the role of infrastructure <sub>p</sub>roviders<sub>,</sub> Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 2- (a) <sub>p</sub>resents the distribution of PQ-TLS–ne<sub>g</sub>otiated domains within the providers responsible for terminating their TLS connections. PQ TLS d<sub>ep</sub>l<sub>oymen</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>are</sub> hi<sub>g</sub>hl<sub>y</sub> <sub>concen</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>among</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>sma</sub>ll <sub>num</sub>b<sub>er</sub> <sub>o</sub>f platforms. In particular, Cloudflare and Fastly together account for nearly 70% of all observed PQ-TLS adoption.

6.3.4 Adoption Alignment. Given this strong infrastructure influence<sub>, w</sub>e examine <sub>w</sub>hether national transition timelines corres<sub>p</sub>ond to observable diferences in PQ-TLS adoption. However, comparin<sub>g</sub> th<sub>ese o</sub>b<sub>se</sub>r<sub>va</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>s w</sub>ith n<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>a</sub>l tr<sub>a</sub>n<sub>s</sub>iti<sub>o</sub>n tim<sub>e</sub>lin<sub>es</sub> r<sub>evea</sub>l<sub>s</sub> no clear evidence that earlier <sub>p</sub>olic<sub>y</sub> tar<sub>g</sub>ets corres<sub>p</sub>ond to hi<sub>g</sub>her observable PQ-TLS deployment. As Figure 3-(b) shows, jurisdictions with earlier transition targets (2030–2031) exhibit lower mean adoption rate (32.4%) than those with later horizons (45.7%), suggesting counterintuitive alignment with policy timeli<sub>nes.</sub>

This a<sub>pp</sub>arent reversal is lar<sub>g</sub>el<sub>y</sub> ex<sub>p</sub>lained b<sub>y</sub> de<sub>p</sub>lo<sub>y</sub>ment com<sub>p</sub>osition. When restrictin<sub>g</sub> the anal<sub>y</sub>sis to <sub>p</sub>otentiall<sub>y</sub> owner-mana<sub>g</sub>ed domains, ado<sub>p</sub>tion remains uniforml<sub>y</sub> low across both <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s (9.6% vs. 8.4%). Likewise, within Cloudflare-hosted domains, which <sub>p</sub>rovi<sup>d</sup>e t<sup>h</sup>e <sup>l</sup>argest common manage<sup>d</sup> p<sup>l</sup>at<sup>f</sup>orm across juris<sup>d</sup>ictions, ado<sub>p</sub>tion rates are hi<sub>g</sub>hl<sub>y</sub> similar across countries (93.5% vs. 94.1%).

Taken together, these results indicate that cross-sector and cross-jurisdiction variation in PQ-TLS adoption aligns more closely with managed infrastructure composition than with sectoral priorities or policy timelines.

Key Takeaways for RQ3: Observable PQ-TLS deployment shows limited alignment with the sectoral priorities and timelines outlined in national PQC policies. Across sectors and jurisdictions, PQ-enabled deployments are predominantly associated with domains served via managed infrastructure platforms, while ownermanaged domains, including government-operated services, remain largely classical.

As our client advertises only TLS 1.3 to enable PQ key exchange, th<sub>ese</sub> f<sub>a</sub>il<sub>ures</sub> <sub>re</sub>fl<sub>ec</sub>t <sub>a</sub> l<sub>ac</sub>k <sub>o</sub>f <sub>suppor</sub>t f<sub>or</sub> <sub>con</sub>t<sub>emporary</sub> TLS <sub>ver-</sub> sions rather than incompatibility with PQC algorithms.

Onl<sub>y</sub> 60,829 domains (19.28%) fail durin<sub>g</sub> the TLS handshake <sub>p</sub>hase where cr<sub>yp</sub>to<sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hic <sub>p</sub>arameters are ne<sub>g</sub>otiated. Even within this subset<sub>,</sub> failures are dominated b<sub>y</sub> <sub>g</sub>eneric ne<sub>g</sub>otiation errors such as no common cipher suite, which account for 49,089 cases (80.7% of handshake-<sub>p</sub>hase failures) and t<sub>yp</sub>icall<sub>y</sub> reflect restrictive <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>con</sub>fi<sub>gura</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub> Oth<sub>er</sub> h<sub>an</sub>d<sub>s</sub>h<sub>a</sub>k<sub>e</sub> f<sub>a</sub>il<sub>ures are compara-</sub> tively rare and include SNI-related rejections (e.g., unrecognized name), server internal errors, malformed message handling (e.g., decode errors), and <sub>p</sub>acket-level violations. Collectivel<sub>y</sub>, these account f<sub>or</sub> l<sub>ess</sub> th<sub>an</sub> 4% <sub>o</sub>f h<sub>an</sub>d<sub>s</sub>h<sub>a</sub>k<sub>e-p</sub>h<sub>ase</sub> f<sub>a</sub>il<sub>ures.</sub>

While certain low-frequency errors, such as decode errors or packet length violations, could theoretically arise from increased handshake messa<sub>g</sub>e sizes, their extremel<sub>y</sub> limited occurrence (less than 0.16% ofhandshake-<sub>p</sub>hase failures), combined with the absence of correlated performance degradation, suggests that MTU-related or fragmentation-induced failures are not a significant barrier to PQ key exchange deployment in current Internet practice.

These results indicate that while early PQ deployments temporarily expose inconsistent configurations across regions, the operational behavior of PQ-TLS remains consistent once configurations are uniformly deployed.

Key Takeaways for RQ4: PQ-TLS deployment exhibits strong operational viability across performance, reliability, and consistency dimensions. At Internet scale, hybrid PQ key exchange introduces no meaningful increase in TLS handshake latency. Compatibility issues are largely unrelated to PQ mechanisms, instead arising from legacy protocol constraints and general network conditions. PQ-TLS behavior remains consistent across regions once uniformly deployed.

These <sub>p</sub>atterns var<sub>y</sub> across <sub>p</sub>roviders<sub>,</sub> reflectin<sub>g p</sub>latform-s<sub>p</sub>ecific <sub>con</sub>fi<sub>gura</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub> F<sub>or</sub> <sub>examp</sub>l<sub>e,</sub> Cl<sub>ou</sub>dfl<sub>are</sub> <sub>ex</sub>hibit<sub>s</sub> hi<sub>g</sub>h<sub>er</sub> l<sub>egacy</sub> <sub>pro-</sub> tocol and cipher support among PQ-TLS domains, Fastly shows lower le<sub>g</sub>ac<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>rotocol su<sub>pp</sub>ort but hi<sub>g</sub>her de<sub>p</sub>recated ci<sub>p</sub>her us-<sub>age, w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> A<sub>mazon</sub> Cl<sub>ou</sub>dF<sub>ron</sub>t <sub>presen</sub>t<sub>s a m</sub>i<sub>xe</sub>d <sub>pro</sub>fil<sub>e.</sub> Th<sub>ese</sub> diferences indicate that managed PQ-TLS deployments largely inherit the TLS compatibility profile of the underlying platform.

6.5.2 Owner-managed domains. For domains classified as potentially owner managed, we compare randomly sample PQ-TLS and classical TLS domains (10,000 each).

In contrast, PQ-TLS adoption among owner-managed domains is associated with modestly stricter configurations, with lower su<sub>pp</sub>ort for le<sub>g</sub>ac<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>rotocol versions (10.5% vs. 11.9%) and de<sub>p</sub>recated ci<sub>p</sub>her suites (46.9% vs. 52.6%). This trend is also <sub>re</sub>fl<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n a</sub> l<sub>ower preva</sub>l<sub>ence o</sub>f <sub>vu</sub>l<sub>nera</sub>bilit<sub>y</sub> i<sub>n</sub>di<sub>ca</sub>t<sub>ors assoc</sub>i<sub>-</sub> ated with BEAST (8.0% vs. 10.0%), BREACH (40.0% vs. 43.1%), and Lucky13 (46.6% vs. 52.3%), alon<sub>g</sub> with reduced su<sub>pp</sub>ort for de<sub>p</sub>- recated stream ci<sub>p</sub>hers such as RC4 (0.04% vs. 0.30%), althou<sub>g</sub>h indicators linked to 64-bit block ci<sub>p</sub>her usa<sub>g</sub>e (Sweet32) remain more common (6.8% vs. 4.5%).

Certificate-related si<sub>g</sub>nals ali<sub>g</sub>n similarl<sub>y,</sub> with hi<sub>g</sub>her OCSP sta-<sub>p</sub>lin<sub>g</sub> rates (12.9% vs. 6.3%), fewer hostname mismatches (5.0% vs. 7.5%) and chain-of-trust issues (2.9% vs. 5.9%).

Taken together, these results indicate that security coevolution during early PQ-TLS deployment is strongly shaped by deployment agency. Among provider-managed domains, PQ-TLS is typically introduced through platforms that combi<sub>ne</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>rong</sub> <sub>cer</sub>tifi<sub>ca</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>au</sub>t<sub>oma</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith b<sub>roa</sub>d b<sub>ac</sub>k<sub>war</sub>d <sub>compa</sub>t<sub>-</sub> ibility. Among owner-managed domains, PQ-TLS adoption more <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ose</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>re</sub>fl<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>s</sub> d<sub>e</sub>lib<sub>era</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>pro</sub>t<sub>oco</sub>l <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>ern</sub>i<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

Key Takeaways for RQ5: PQ-TLS adoption does not imply a uniform improvement in protocol-level security. Provider-managed deployments inherit platform-level configurations that combine stronger certificate practices with greater retention of legacy protocol features, while owner-managed deployments are associated with modestly stricter TLS configurations.

## 9 Conclusion

Thi<sub>s paper presen</sub>t<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t I<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erne</sub>t<sub>-sca</sub>l<sub>e measuremen</sub>t <sub>s</sub>t<sub>u</sub>d<sub>y o</sub>f PQ-TLS deployment in HTTPS. By analyzing more than 2 billion TLS h<sub>an</sub>d<sub>s</sub>h<sub>a</sub>k<sub>es across one m</sub>illi<sub>on</sub> d<sub>oma</sub>i<sub>ns, co</sub>ll<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d f<sub>rom</sub> 11 <sub>g</sub>loball<sub>y</sub> distributed vanta<sub>g</sub>e <sub>p</sub>oints<sub>,</sub> we <sub>p</sub>rovide an em<sub>p</sub>irical view of how PQC is transitioning from standardization into real-world <sup>d</sup>e<sub>p</sub><sup>l</sup>o<sub>y</sub>ment.

O<sub>ur measuremen</sub>t<sub>s revea</sub>l li<sub>m</sub>it<sub>e</sub>d <sub>correspon</sub>d<sub>ence</sub> b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween na-</sub> tional <sub>p</sub>olic<sub>y</sub> ex<sub>p</sub>ectations and earl<sub>y</sub> de<sub>p</sub>lo<sub>y</sub>ment <sub>p</sub>atterns. While nearly half of the stable domain panel prioritize PQ key exchange b<sub>y</sub> M<sub>arc</sub>h 2026<sub>,</sub> d<sub>ep</sub>l<sub>oymen</sub>t i<sub>s</sub> <sub>narrow</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>concen</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>aroun</sub>d <sub>a</sub> sin<sub>g</sub>le h<sub>y</sub>brid construction, X25519MLKEM768. In contrast, we find no verified deployment of PQ signature algorithms for TLS authentication. PQ-TLS adoption is overwhelmingly driven by managed i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>ras</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ure</sub> <sub>prov</sub>id<sub>ers,</sub> <sub>w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> <sub>owner-manage</sub>d <sub>an</sub>d <sub>governmen</sub>t<sub>-</sub> <sub>opera</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d d<sub>oma</sub>i<sub>ns</sub> l<sub>arge</sub>l<sub>y</sub> d<sub>e</sub>f<sub>au</sub>lt t<sub>o</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l<sub>.</sub> A<sub>cross</sub> <sub>sec</sub>t<sub>ors</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>coun</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>es, o</sub>b<sub>serva</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> d<sub>ep</sub>l<sub>oymen</sub>t <sub>s</sub>h<sub>ows</sub> li<sub>m</sub>it<sub>e</sub>d <sub>correspon</sub>d<sub>ence</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>p</sub>olic<sub>y</sub> timelines and <sub>p</sub>riorities.

At the same time, our results demonstrate that PQ-TLS is operati<sub>ona</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>v</sub>i<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>a</sub>t I<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erne</sub>t <sub>sca</sub>l<sub>e.</sub> H<sub>y</sub>b<sub>r</sub>id k<sub>ey</sub> <sub>exc</sub>h<sub>ange</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>d<sub>uces</sub> <sub>no</sub> meaningful increase in handshake latency, and PQ mechanisms do not result in observable compatibility failures. However, PQ adoption does not necessaril<sub>y</sub> coincide <sub>w</sub>ith broader TLS hardenin<sub>g,</sub> <sub>as</sub> d<sub>oma</sub>i<sub>ns</sub> <sub>o</sub>ft<sub>en</sub> <sub>re</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub> l<sub>egacy</sub> <sub>pro</sub>t<sub>oco</sub>l <sub>vers</sub>i<sub>ons</sub> <sub>an</sub>d d<sub>epreca</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sup>ci</sup>p<sup>her</sup> <sup>su</sup>pp<sup>ort.</sup>

These findings suggest that successful PQC transition will re-<sub>qu</sub>i<sub>re more</sub> th<sub>an a</sub>l<sub>gor</sub>ith<sub>m s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>di<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on an</sub>d <sub>ca</sub>l<sub>en</sub>d<sub>ar-</sub>b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>m</sub>i<sub>-</sub> <sub>gra</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> P<sub>o</sub>li<sub>c</sub>i<sub>es s</sub>h<sub>ou</sub>ld <sub>accoun</sub>t f<sub>or</sub> th<sub>e cen</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>l <sub>ro</sub>l<sub>e o</sub>f i<sub>n-</sub> frastructure <sub>p</sub>roviders<sub>,</sub> su<sub>pp</sub>ort owner-mana<sub>g</sub>ed s<sub>y</sub>stems with concrete de<sub>p</sub>lo<sub>y</sub>ment <sub>g</sub>uidance and toolin<sub>g,</sub> and incor<sub>p</sub>orate em<sub>p</sub>irical <sub>p</sub>rotocol-level measurements into <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>g</sub>ress trackin<sub>g</sub>. Without this operational focus, PQC migration may become visible in aggregate statistics while remainin<sub>g</sub> uneven across sectors<sub>,</sub> de<sub>p</sub>endent on <sub>a sma</sub>ll <sub>num</sub>b<sub>er o</sub>f <sub>p</sub>l<sub>a</sub>tf<sub>orms, an</sub>d <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y par</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>y a</sub>li<sub>gne</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ith th<sub>e</sub> <sub>secu</sub>rit<sub>y ou</sub>t<sub>co</sub>m<sub>es</sub> tr<sub>a</sub>n<sub>s</sub>iti<sub>o</sub>n <sub>po</sub>li<sub>c</sub>i<sub>es a</sub>im t<sub>o ac</sub>hi<sub>eve.</sub>

## C.3 Validation and Sensitivity Analysis

## E Protocol-level Security Depth: Labeling Criteria

This a<sub>pp</sub>endix documents the labelin<sub>g</sub> criteria and test semantics used in our <sub>p</sub>rotocol-level securit<sub>y</sub> de<sub>p</sub>th anal<sub>y</sub>sis (RQ4). ## E.1 Protocol Version Support

A domain is labeled as s<sub>upp</sub>ortin<sub>g</sub> an obsolete <sub>p</sub>rotocol version if it ofers any of the following during protocol negotiation, regardless <sub>o</sub>f d<sub>e</sub>f<sub>au</sub>lt <sub>pre</sub>f<sub>erence:</sub>

Domains that <sub>p</sub>refer TLS 1.3 b<sub>u</sub>t retain s<sub>upp</sub>ort f<sub>or</sub> <sub>o</sub>ld<sub>er</sub> <sub>vers</sub>i<sub>ons</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>s</sub>till <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>e</sub>d <sub>as</sub> <sub>o</sub>f<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>o</sub>b<sub>so</sub>l<sub>e</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>pro</sub>t<sub>oco</sub>l<sub>s,</sub> <sub>as</sub> <sub>suc</sub>h <sub>con</sub>fi<sub>gura</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> <sub>may</sub> <sub>preserve</sub> d<sub>owngra</sub>d<sub>e</sub> <sub>pa</sub>th<sub>s.</sub>

## E.2 Cipher Suite Classification

Ci<sub>p</sub>h<sub>er-re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d l<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s are ass</sub>i<sub>gne</sub>d b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>on a</sub>d<sub>ver</sub>ti<sub>se</sub>d <sub>c</sub>i<sub>p</sub>h<sub>er su</sub>it<sub>e</sub> su<sub>pp</sub>ort accordin<sub>g</sub> to O<sub>p</sub>enSSL<sup>7</sup> cate<sub>g</sub>orisation. A domain is fla<sub>gg</sub>ed if it ofers an<sub>y</sub> ci<sub>p</sub>her suites fallin<sub>g</sub> into the followin<sub>g</sub> cate<sub>g</sub>ories:

• NULL Ciphers: Provide no encryption.

• aNULL Ciphers: Provide encryption but no authentication, <sub>ena</sub>bli<sub>ng man-</sub>i<sub>n-</sub>th<sub>e-m</sub>iddl<sub>e a</sub>tt<sub>ac</sub>k<sub>s.</sub>

• Export Ciphers: Suites with intentionally weakened 40–56 bit k<sub>eys,</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>d<sub>uce</sub>d f<sub>or</sub> <sub>expor</sub>t <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>l <sub>comp</sub>li<sub>ance</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>now</sub> t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>v</sub>i<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> b<sub>ro</sub>k<sub>en.</sub>

• Low Ciphers: Use outdated 64-bit block ciphers.

• Obsoleted Ciphers: Suites formally deprecated by stan d<sub>ar</sub>d<sub>s</sub> b<sub>o</sub>di<sub>es or remove</sub>d f<sub>rom mo</sub>d<sub>ern</sub> TLS <sub>pro</sub>t<sub>oco</sub>l<sub>s.</sub>

• 3DES/IDEA Ciphers: Legacy block ciphers with outdated <sub>cons</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>ti<sub>ons or</sub> li<sub>m</sub>it<sub>e</sub>d bl<sub>oc</sub>k <sub>s</sub>i<sub>zes.</sub>

• Strong-NoFS Ciphers: Suites that employ strong encryption but lack forward secrec<sub>y,</sub> ex<sub>p</sub>osin<sub>g p</sub>ast sessions if lon<sub>g</sub>- term ke<sub>y</sub>s are ever com<sub>p</sub>romised.

Ci<sub>p</sub>her availabilit<sub>y</sub> is evaluated inde<sub>p</sub>endentl<sub>y</sub> of ne<sub>g</sub>otiation <sub>p</sub>ref erence.

## E.3 Known TLS Vulnerability Indicators

We flag domains for potential exposure indicators associated with <sub>we</sub>ll<sub>-</sub>k<sub>nown</sub> TLS <sub>wea</sub>k<sub>nesses.</sub> Th<sub>ese</sub> l<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s</sub> d<sub>o</sub> <sub>no</sub>t i<sub>mp</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>exp</sub>l<sub>o</sub>it<sub>a</sub>bil it<sub>y,</sub> <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> <sub>re</sub>l<sub>evan</sub>t <sub>pro</sub>t<sub>oco</sub>l f<sub>ea</sub>t<sub>ures</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>presen</sub>t<sub>.</sub> I<sub>n</sub>di<sub>ca</sub>t<sub>ors</sub> i<sub>nc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>d<sub>e:</sub>

• POODLE: SSLv3 with CBC ci<sub>p</sub>her su<sub>pp</sub>ort.

• SWEET32: su<sub>pp</sub>ort for 64-bit block ci<sub>p</sub>hers.

• FREAK: EXPORT-<sub>g</sub>rade RSA ci<sub>p</sub>her su<sub>pp</sub>ort.

• BEAST: TLS 1.0 with CBC ci<sub>p</sub>hers (even if hi<sub>g</sub>her versions are also su<sub>pp</sub>orted).

• Lucky13: su<sub>pp</sub>ort for CBC-mode ci<sub>p</sub>hers under TLS.

• RC4: RC4 ci<sub>p</sub>her su<sub>pp</sub>ort.

• LOGJAM: DH EXPORT ci<sub>p</sub>her su<sub>pp</sub>ort.

• BREACH: HTTP com<sub>p</sub>ression enabled, detected via adver tised content-encodin<sub>g</sub> headers on a sin<sub>g</sub>le beni<sub>g</sub>n re<sub>q</sub>uest t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e roo</sub>t <sub>pa</sub>th<sub>.</sub>

## E.4 Certificate Properties

C<sub>er</sub>tifi<sub>ca</sub>t<sub>e-re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>ssues</sub> <sub>are</sub> id<sub>en</sub>tifi<sub>e</sub>d <sub>us</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>cr</sub>it<sub>er</sub>i<sub>a:</sub>

• Certificate expiration: the leaf certificate is expired at scan time.

• Hostname mismatch: the certificate does not correctly <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h th<sub>e supp</sub>li<sub>e</sub>d d<sub>oma</sub>i<sub>n name,</sub> i<sub>nc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>di<sub>ng cases w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>hi<sub>n re</sub>li<sub>es so</sub>l<sub>e</sub>l <sub>on</sub> CN <sub>or w</sub>ild<sub>car</sub>d b<sub>e</sub>h<sub>av</sub>i<sub>or.</sub>

• Chain of trust issues: including incomplete chains, expired i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erme</sub>di<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es, se</sub>lf<sub>-s</sub>i<sub>gne</sub>d <sub>cer</sub>tifi<sub>ca</sub>t<sub>es, or se</sub>lf<sub>-s</sub>i<sub>gne</sub>d <sub>cer</sub>tifi<sub>-</sub> <sub>ca</sub>t<sub>e au</sub>th<sub>o</sub>riti<sub>es w</sub>ithin th<sub>e c</sub>h<sub>a</sub>in<sub>.</sub>

## E.5 OCSP Stapling

OCSP sta<sub>p</sub>lin<sub>g</sub> is eval<sub>u</sub>ated based on server behavior d<sub>u</sub>rin<sub>g</sub> the TLS handshake. Domains are classified as not oferin<sub>g</sub> OCSP sta<sub>p</sub>lin<sub>g</sub> if:

• No sta<sub>p</sub>led OCSP res<sub>p</sub>onse is <sub>p</sub>rovided, or

• The sta<sub>p</sub>led res<sub>p</sub>onse contains an OCSP error (e.<sub>g</sub>., unauthorized).
